#### What the text states

- **The October 2023 interim final rule revises ECCN 3A090.a to control ICs with a total processing performance of 4800 or more, or of 1600 or more with a performance density of 5.92 or more, and states that the performance density parameter is meant to prevent buying many smaller datacenter AI chips that combined would equal restricted chips.** `cpo-ch-bis-acr-2023-3a090-thresholds`
  2023-10-25 · published · US · `states_provision`
  _Date:_ Provision effective 17 November 2023.
  _Limits:_ States the rule’s thresholds and stated purpose as described in its preamble; it does not show which products fall under them. The 3A090.b parameters and later revisions (December 2024, January 2025) are recorded separately or not at all.

  > “The revised 3A090.a control parameter will control ICs with one or more digital processing units having either: (1) a ‘total processing performance’ of 4800 or more, or (2) a ‘total processing performance’ of 1600 or more and a ‘performance density’ of 5.92 or more.”  
  > [Implementation of Additional Export Controls: Certain Advanced Computing Items; Supercomputer and Semiconductor End Use; Updates and Corrections (88 FR 73458)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2023-10-25/pdf/2023-23055.pdf), 88 FR 73474, preamble (PDF p. 17)
  > “A performance density parameter prevents the workaround of simply purchasing a larger number of smaller datacenter AI chips which, if combined, would be equally powerful as restricted chips.”  
  > [Implementation of Additional Export Controls: Certain Advanced Computing Items; Supercomputer and Semiconductor End Use; Updates and Corrections (88 FR 73458)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2023-10-25/pdf/2023-23055.pdf), 88 FR 73474, preamble (PDF p. 17)

- **The December 2024 interim final rule adds controls on certain high bandwidth memory, as new ECCN 3A090.c, stating that such memory provides capacity and bandwidth needed for advanced AI models and supercomputing.** `cpo-ch-bis-fdp-hbm-2024-hbm`
  2024-12-05 · published · US · `states_provision`
  _Date:_ Compliance date for the HBM controls: 31 December 2024.
  _Limits:_ Records the provision and the rule’s stated rationale; it does not state the technical parameters of 3A090.c (not quoted) or show their effect on supply.

  > “adding new controls for certain high bandwidth memory important for advanced computing”  
  > [Foreign-Produced Direct Product Rule Additions, and Refinements to Controls for Advanced Computing and Semiconductor Manufacturing Items (89 FR 96790)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-12-05/pdf/2024-28270.pdf), 89 FR 96790, SUMMARY
  > “memory (HBM) commodities that provide necessary memory capacity and bandwidth needed for advanced artificial intelligence (AI) models and supercomputing applications”  
  > [Foreign-Produced Direct Product Rule Additions, and Refinements to Controls for Advanced Computing and Semiconductor Manufacturing Items (89 FR 96790)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-12-05/pdf/2024-28270.pdf), 89 FR 96792, preamble (PDF p. 3)
  > “Addition of HBM controls, including addition of new ECCN 3A090.c and License Exceptions HBM”  
  > [Foreign-Produced Direct Product Rule Additions, and Refinements to Controls for Advanced Computing and Semiconductor Manufacturing Items (89 FR 96790)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-12-05/pdf/2024-28270.pdf), 89 FR 96793, overview (PDF p. 4)

- **The January 2025 foundry due-diligence rule allows reliance on an attestation that the aggregated approximated transistor count of the final packaged IC is below 30 billion transistors, or, for ICs without high bandwidth memory, below 35 billion in 2027 and 40 billion later, as one route around the new licence requirement, alongside lists of approved IC designers and OSAT companies.** `cpo-ch-bis-foundry-dd-2025-transistor-attestation`
  2025-01-16 · published · US · `states_provision`
  _Limits:_ A paraphrase of a conditional provision with several branches; the exact years for the 40 billion threshold are cut off in the quotation and should be read in the rule. It does not show which chips or foundries were affected.

  > “the ‘‘aggregated approximated transistor count’’ of the final packaged IC is below 30 billion transistors, or (b) the final packaged IC does not contain high bandwidth memory (HBM) and that the ‘‘aggregated approximated transistor count’’ of the final packaged IC is below (i) 35 billion transistors”  
  > [Implementation of Additional Due Diligence Measures for Advanced Computing Integrated Circuits; Amendments and Clarifications; and Extension of Comment Period (90 FR 5298)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-01-16/pdf/2025-00711.pdf), 90 FR 5305, preamble (PDF p. 8)
  > “Approved IC designers (listed in supplement no. 6 to part 740), (ii) Approved ‘‘OSAT’’ companies (listed in supplement no. 7 to part 740)”  
  > [Implementation of Additional Due Diligence Measures for Advanced Computing Integrated Circuits; Amendments and Clarifications; and Extension of Comment Period (90 FR 5298)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-01-16/pdf/2025-00711.pdf), 90 FR 5301, preamble (PDF p. 4)

- **The BIS guidance of 13 May 2025 lists the Huawei Ascend 910B, 910C and 910D as illustrative PRC 3A090 ICs subject to the presumption that General Prohibition 10 restrictions apply, and states that the list is not exhaustive.** `cpo-ch-bis-gp10-guidance-ascend-list`
  2025-05-13 · issued · US · `states_provision`

- **The July 2026 rule states that the UAE Government and approved commercial entities will have licence-free access to advanced computing items, consistent with the May 2025 U.S.-UAE Artificial Intelligence Cooperation framework, while licence requirements are maintained for other advanced computing exports to or within the UAE.** `cpo-ch-bis-uae-2026-advanced-computing`
  2026-07-10 · effective · Gulf · `states_provision`
  _Limits:_ Access is limited to the UAE Government, approved UAE commercial entities and U.S.-headquartered AI entities listed in a supplement; the list of approved entities is not recorded. The May 2025 framework it cites is not recorded here (see omissions).

  > “The UAE Government and approved commercial entities will also have license-free access to advanced computing items, consistent with the May 2025 U.S.-UAE Artificial Intelligence Cooperation framework”  
  > [Enhanced Favorable Treatment for the United Arab Emirates Under the Export Administration Regulations (91 FR 43034)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-07-14/pdf/2026-14132.pdf), 91 FR 43034, SUMMARY
  > “maintains license requirements for advanced computing items destined to or within the UAE, except for UAE Government agencies as well as approved UAE commercial entities and U.S.-headquartered AI entities identified in supplement no. 8 to part 740”  
  > [Enhanced Favorable Treatment for the United Arab Emirates Under the Export Administration Regulations (91 FR 43034)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-07-14/pdf/2026-14132.pdf), 91 FR 43035, preamble

- **The July 2026 BIS rule on the UAE states that, although § 742.6(a)(6)(iii)(A) of the EAR specifies a worldwide licence requirement for certain advanced computing items, BIS, consistent with guidance issued on 13 May 2025 and 31 May 2026, is only enforcing it for destinations in Country Groups D:1, D:4 or D:5 and for entities headquartered in Country Group D:5 or Macau.** `cpo-ch-bis-uae-2026-diffusion-enforcement`
  2026-07-14 · published · US · `states_provision` · adds to dataset record `fl-us-bis-ai-diffusion-rescission-announced`
  _Date:_ Date of the Federal Register issue; the rule is effective 10 July 2026. The rule cites the 13 May 2025 guidance by the address of BIS's press release announcing the rescission of the AI diffusion rule.
  _Limits:_ A statement of enforcement practice in the preamble of a 2026 rule, not a rescission rule: it says the worldwide requirement is still specified in the EAR and enforced only for the listed groups. It bears on the dataset's record of the announced rescission (fl-us-bis-ai-diffusion-rescission-announced), which stays an announcement; no Federal Register rule formalising the rescission was found in this pack's search. The 31 May 2026 guidance was not retrieved.

### US government equity stake in Intel (August 2025)

#### Announcements, plans and agreements

- **Intel’s Form 8-K for 22 August 2025 states that the warrants issued to the Department of Commerce are exercisable only if Intel ceases to own at least 51% of its foundry business, expire on the fifth anniversary of closing and carry an exercise price of $20.00 per share.** `cpo-ch-intel-usg-warrant`
  2025-08-22 · signed · US · proposed predicate `granted_equity_right`
  _Date:_ Terms of the agreement entered on 22 August 2025; warrants issued at closing on 27 August 2025.
  _Figures and stated dates:_ quantity up to 240516150 shares (as worded: 'up to 240,516,150 shares of common stock') (measures: shares); stage: contracted
  _Limits:_ A conditional right; it does not show that the condition has occurred or that any warrant has been exercised.

  > “The Warrants will be exercisable only if, and from the date on which, the Company ceases to directly or indirectly own at least 51% of its foundry business, and will expire on the fifth anniversary of the Closing Date.”  
  > [Intel Corporation Form 8-K, date of report August 22, 2025 (Warrant and Common Stock Agreement with the U.S. Department of Commerce)](https://www.intc.com/filings-reports/all-sec-filings/content/0000050863-25-000129/intc-20250822.htm), Item 1.01, warrant paragraph
  > “an exercise price of $20.00 per share”  
  > [Intel Corporation Form 8-K, date of report August 22, 2025 (Warrant and Common Stock Agreement with the U.S. Department of Commerce)](https://www.intc.com/filings-reports/all-sec-filings/content/0000050863-25-000129/intc-20250822.htm), Item 1.01, warrant paragraph

- **Intel reported in a Form 8-K that on 22 August 2025 it entered into a Warrant and Common Stock Agreement with the U.S. Department of Commerce under which the US Government agreed to make $8,869,800,000 of disbursements and Intel agreed to issue up to 433,323,000 shares of common stock and warrants.** `cpo-ch-usg-intel-agreement`
  2025-08-22 · signed · US · proposed predicate `government_award`
  _Date:_ Date of entry into the agreement as stated in the 8-K; the 8-K is signed 25 August 2025.
  _Figures and stated dates:_ quantity up to 433323000 shares of Intel common stock (as worded: 'up to 433,323,000 shares') (measures: shares); amount 8,869,800,000 USD (transaction); stage: contracted
  _Limits:_ Records the agreement as Intel describes it in its filing. The disbursements comprise the acceleration of $5,695,000,000 under Intel’s existing CHIPS Direct Funding Agreement and $3,174,800,000 under the Secure Enclave program; the 8-K itself does not mention AI (the AI reference is in the accompanying release, cpo-ch-usg-intel-announced). It is not a new appropriation and does not show funds received (see cpo-ch-usg-intel-closing).

  > the securities to be issued comprise the Shares (including escrowed shares) and warrants exercisable to purchase up to 240,516,150 shares of common stock, to be issued on the Closing Date. _(paraphrase)_

- **Intel’s release of 22 August 2025 announced that the US government would make an $8.9 billion investment in Intel common stock, purchasing 433.3 million shares at $20.47 per share (stated as equivalent to a 9.9 percent stake), funded by $5.7 billion of unpaid CHIPS Act grants and $3.2 billion awarded under the Secure Enclave program; the release quotes Commerce Secretary Lutnick saying the administration “remains committed to reinforcing our country’s dominance in artificial intelligence”.** `cpo-ch-usg-intel-announced`

## Orbital compute

**Propositions (arguments for and against orbital compute)**

| Code | Proposition                                                                                                                                                                                                | Earliest located (in this pack’s searches)                                                                                             | Texts stating it | Texts contesting or limiting it |
| ---- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------: | ------------------------------: |
| OC1  | Solar power in suitable orbits is more continuous or more abundant than on Earth, and this is given as a reason to place computing there.                                                                  | Why we should train AI in space (2024-09) `cpo-orb-oc1-lumen-2024`                                                                     |                3 |                               0 |
| OC2  | Rejecting heat by radiation to space lets an orbital data centre avoid the water use, chillers or cooling energy of terrestrial data centres.                                                              | Thales Alenia Space reveals results of ASCEND feasibility study on space data centers (2024-06-27) `cpo-orb-oc2-ascend-2024`           |                2 |                               1 |
| OC3  | Constraints on building or powering data centres on Earth (land, permitting, grid connection, power supply, water or local impact) limit their growth, and placing compute in orbit is said to avoid them. | Why we should train AI in space (2024-09) `cpo-orb-oc3-lumen-2024`                                                                     |                3 |                               0 |
| OC4  | The economic viability of orbital compute depends on launch prices falling, stated with or without a price threshold per kilogram.                                                                         | Why we should train AI in space (2024-09) `cpo-orb-oc4-lumen-2024`                                                                     |                2 |                               0 |
| OC5  | Orbital data centres can have lower lifecycle greenhouse-gas emissions than terrestrial ones, with or without stated conditions (such as a less emissive launcher).                                        | Thales Alenia Space reveals results of ASCEND feasibility study on space data centers (2024-06-27) `cpo-orb-oc5-ascend-2024`           |                2 |                               1 |
| OC6  | Because heat in orbit can be rejected only by radiation, thermal management (for example the required radiator area) is a significant engineering or cost constraint on orbital compute.                   | Exploring a space-based, scalable AI infrastructure system design (2025-11-04) `cpo-orb-oc6-suncatcher-blog-2025`                      |                3 |                               1 |
| OC7  | Ionizing radiation in orbit degrades or disrupts computing hardware, requiring shielding, redundancy, error correction or hardened parts.                                                                  | Why we should train AI in space (2024-09) `cpo-orb-oc7-lumen-2024`                                                                     |                2 |                               1 |
| OC8  | Failed hardware in orbit cannot practically be repaired or replaced by technicians as on Earth, so failures must be met by redundancy, module replacement or launches.                                     | Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (2025-11-22) `cpo-orb-oc8-suncatcher-paper-2025`         |                1 |                               2 |
| OC9  | At current or assumed launch and spacecraft prices, computing in orbit costs more than equivalent computing on Earth.                                                                                      | Orbital Data Centers: Spacecraft Constraints and Economic Viability (2026-04-29) `cpo-orb-oc9-turyshev-2026`                           |                2 |                               1 |
| OC10 | Links between orbital compute and users or data on the ground (bandwidth, latency, ground stations) limit which workloads orbital compute can serve.                                                       | Exploring a space-based, scalable AI infrastructure system design (2025-11-04) `cpo-orb-oc10-suncatcher-blog-2025`                     |                2 |                               0 |
| OC11 | Large orbital-data-centre constellations would harm astronomical observation or the night sky, or raise collision, debris or atmospheric risks.                                                            | Comment of the Accademia Nazionale dei Lincei on SpaceX’s Application for Orbital Data Centers (2026-03-03) `cpo-orb-oc11-lincei-2026` |                2 |                               2 |

### ASCEND study (European Commission, Thales Alenia Space)

#### Announcements, plans and agreements

- **Thales Alenia Space announced on 14 November 2022 that the European Commission had chosen it to lead the ASCEND feasibility study for data centres in orbit, within Horizon Europe.** `cpo-orb-ascend-study-chosen`
  2022-11-14 · announced · EU · proposed predicate `government_award`
  _Figures and stated dates:_ stage: announced
  _Limits:_ Thales Alenia Space's announcement; no Commission text or amount was retrieved. Before the pack's 2023 start, recorded because the 2024 results depend on it.

  > “has been chosen by the European Commission to lead the ASCEND (Advanced Space Cloud for European Net zero emission and Data sovereignty) feasibility study for data centers in orbit”

### OC1 Orbital solar supply

#### Arguments

- **The Lumen Orbit white paper (September 2024) argues that data centres in orbit can use solar power around the clock, unhindered by night, weather and the atmosphere.** `cpo-orb-oc1-lumen-2024`
  2024-09 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located
  _Limits:_ Earliest located among the texts retrieved for this strand on 2026-10-28 (company, agency, scientific-society and arXiv texts on orbital compute, 2019-2026, found by web discovery searches and direct retrieval); not a claim that no earlier text exists. Date as printed on v1.01. The ASCEND announcement of November 2022 proposed solar-powered orbital stations using energy produced outside the atmosphere, but without stating the comparison, so it does not meet the criterion as worded.

  > “Data centers in space can utilize high-intensity 24/7 solar power unhindered by day/night cycles, weather, and atmospheric losses (attenuation).”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 2, "Reduced Operating Expenses"

- **Google's Suncatcher post (November 2025) states that in the right orbit a solar panel can be up to eight times more productive than on Earth and produce power nearly continuously.** `cpo-orb-oc1-suncatcher-2025`
  2025-11-04 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: supporting
  _Limits:_ A proponent's statement; the factor is Google's, citing its paper.

  > “In the right orbit, a solar panel can be up to 8 times more productive than on earth, and produce power nearly continuously, reducing the need for batteries.”  
  > [Exploring a space-based, scalable AI infrastructure system design](https://research.google/blog/exploring-a-space-based-scalable-ai-infrastructure-system-design/), paragraph 3

- **SpaceX's update of 2 February 2026 argues that satellites directly harnessing near-constant solar power will transform the ability to scale compute.** `cpo-orb-oc1-spacex-2026`
  2026-02-02 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: supporting
  _Limits:_ Signed company letter; the argument accompanies the acquisition announcement.
  > “By directly harnessing near-constant solar power with little operating or maintenance costs, these satellites will transform our ability to scale compute.”  
  > [xAI joins SpaceX to Accelerate Humanity’s Future](https://content.spacex.com/api/spacex-website/updates), paragraph 5

### OC2 Radiative cooling advantage

#### Arguments

- **The ASCEND results release (June 2024) states that space data centres would not require water to cool them.** `cpo-orb-oc2-ascend-2024`
  2024-06-27 · published · EU · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located
  _Limits:_ Earliest located among the texts retrieved for this strand on 2026-10-28 (company, agency, scientific-society and arXiv texts on orbital compute, 2019-2026, found by web discovery searches and direct retrieval); not a claim that no earlier text exists. It states the water point only; it says nothing of radiators or cooling energy. The study report was not read.

  > “space data centers would not require water to cool them”  
  > [Thales Alenia Space reveals results of ASCEND feasibility study on space data centers](https://www.thalesaleniaspace.com/en/press-releases/thales-alenia-space-reveals-results-ascend-feasibility-study-space-data-centers-0), paragraph 4

- **The Lumen Orbit white paper (September 2024) argues that orbital data centres can lower cooling costs by passive radiative cooling to space.** `cpo-orb-oc2-lumen-2024`
  2024-09 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: supporting
  _Limits:_ Proponent; the same paper states the radiator requirement (cpo-orb-oc6-lumen-2024).

  > “Orbital data centers can leverage lower cooling costs using passive radiative cooling in space to directly achieve low coolant temperatures.”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 2, "Why Data Centers in Space?"

- **Cavalier's IEEE Spectrum article (June 2026) calls "free cooling" perhaps the biggest misconception about computing in space, because without an atmosphere only radiation can remove heat.** `cpo-orb-oc2-cavalier-2026`
  2026-06-11 · published · Other · `contests_proposition` · role: contesting
  _Limits:_ An analyst's signed argument (ABI Research); it contests the cost advantage, not the absence of water use.
  > “Free cooling is perhaps the biggest misconception.”  
  > [Why Thermodynamics Rules Future Orbital Data Centers](https://spectrum.ieee.org/orbital-data-centers-heat), paragraph 4
  > “Space is cold, but it also has no atmosphere. That means the best heat-removal mechanisms, conduction and convection, are off the table. The only option is radiation.”  
  > [Why Thermodynamics Rules Future Orbital Data Centers](https://spectrum.ieee.org/orbital-data-centers-heat), paragraph 4

### OC3 Terrestrial constraints

#### Arguments

  2024-09 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located

The 2022 ASCEND announcement cites terrestrial data centres' environmental footprint, not constraints on building them.

### OC4 Launch-price condition

#### Arguments

- **The Lumen Orbit white paper (September 2024) assumes a long-term launch price of about $30 per kilogram and argues that at such prices launch is no longer a primary cost driver.** `cpo-orb-oc4-lumen-2024`
  2024-09 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located
  _Limits:_ Earliest located among the texts retrieved for this strand on 2026-10-28 (company, agency, scientific-society and arXiv texts on orbital compute, 2019-2026, found by web discovery searches and direct retrieval); not a claim that no earlier text exists. The $30/kg figure is the paper's expectation for reusable heavy-lift vehicles, not a quoted price.

  > “this translates to approximately $30 per kilogram … At these price points, launch costs are no longer a primary cost driver for orbital data centers.”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 10, "Launch"

- **The Suncatcher paper (November 2025) argues that if launch to LEO reaches $200 per kilogram, launch cost amortised over spacecraft life could be roughly comparable to terrestrial data-centre energy costs per kW.** `cpo-orb-oc4-suncatcher-paper-2025`
  2025-11-22 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: supporting
  _Limits:_ A conditional by proponents; the paper projects such prices by about the mid-2030s under a learning-curve assumption and says it is not a full economic feasibility study.
  > “if launch costs to LEO reach $200/kg, then the cost of launch amortized over spacecraft lifetime could be roughly comparable to data center energy costs, on a per-kW basis”  
  > [Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (arXiv:2511.19468v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.19468v1), PDF p. 6, section on launch costs
  > “a learning curve analysis suggests launch to low-Earth orbit (LEO) may reach ≲$200/kg by the mid-2030s”  
  > [Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (arXiv:2511.19468v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.19468v1), PDF p. 1, abstract · context
  > “not intended as a comprehensive economic feasibility study”  
  > [Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (arXiv:2511.19468v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.19468v1), PDF p. 10 · context

### OC5 Lifecycle emissions

#### Arguments

- **The ASCEND results release (June 2024) states that, to significantly reduce the CO2 emissions of data processing and storage, space data centres would require a launcher ten times less emissive over its lifecycle.** `cpo-orb-oc5-ascend-2024`
  2024-06-27 · published · EU · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located
  _Limits:_ Earliest located among the texts retrieved for this strand on 2026-10-28 (company, agency, scientific-society and arXiv texts on orbital compute, 2019-2026, found by web discovery searches and direct retrieval); not a claim that no earlier text exists. A conditional finding as the release states it; the study report and its assumptions were not read. The November 2022 announcement had posed the same question as an objective to assess, without asserting it.

  > “the results of the study estimate that such space infrastructures would require the development of a launcher ten times less emissive over its entire lifecycle”  
  > [Thales Alenia Space reveals results of ASCEND feasibility study on space data centers](https://www.thalesaleniaspace.com/en/press-releases/thales-alenia-space-reveals-results-ascend-feasibility-study-space-data-centers-0), paragraph 4

- **NVIDIA's post (October 2025) quotes Starcloud's chief executive Philip Johnston as saying that, apart from launch, orbital data centres would bring tenfold carbon-dioxide savings over their life compared with terrestrial power.** `cpo-orb-oc5-nvidia-starcloud-2025`
  2025-10-15 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: supporting
  _Limits:_ A chief executive's quoted claim relayed by a partner; no basis is given.

  > “then there will be 10x carbon-dioxide savings over the life of the data center”  
  > [How Starcloud Is Bringing Data Centers to Outer Space](https://blogs.nvidia.com/blog/starcloud/), paragraph 3 (quotation of Philip Johnston)

- **The Accademia dei Lincei's FCC comment (March 2026) argues that launching about a million tons a year, as SpaceX considers, would deposit a million tons of fuel a year in the stratosphere and mesosphere with significant environmental consequences.** `cpo-orb-oc5-lincei-2026`
  2026-03-03 · published · EU · `contests_proposition` · role: contesting
  _Limits:_ Addresses the SpaceX proposal's launch rate rather than lifecycle accounting in general; recorded as contesting because it names an atmospheric cost of the launches.
  > “launching in LEO orbits of the order of a million tons per year, as considered by SpaceX, would deposit million tons per year of fuel (mostly kerosene and methane) in the stratosphere and mesosphere with significant environmental consequences”  
  > [Comment on SpaceX’s Application for Orbital Data Centers, ICFS File No. SAT-LOA-20260108-00016](https://www.lincei.it/sites/default/files/2026/ANL_Comment_to_FCC__Proposal_SpaceX_1million_satelllites_04_signed_2b.pdf), p. 2, list item 7

### OC6 Heat-rejection limit

#### Arguments

- **The Lumen Orbit white paper (September 2024) states that a gigawatt-scale orbital data centre would need radiators dissipating gigawatts of heat, by far the largest deployed in space, while estimating them at under half the solar-array size.** `cpo-orb-oc6-lumen-2024`
  2024-09 · published · US · `bears_on_proposition` · role: context
  _Limits:_ Context from a proponent: it states the radiator requirement but presents it as manageable, so it does not meet the criterion as a constraint.

  > “the data center will require radiators capable of radiatively dissipating gigawatts of thermal load”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 8, "Thermal Management"
  > “by far the largest radiators deployed in space”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 8, "Thermal Management"
  > “these radiators need to be less than half the size of the solar arrays”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 8, "Thermal Management"

- **Google's Suncatcher post (November 2025) names thermal management among the significant engineering challenges that remain for space-based ML compute.** `cpo-orb-oc6-suncatcher-blog-2025`
  2025-11-04 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located, contesting
  _Limits:_ Earliest located among the texts retrieved for this strand on 2026-10-28 (company, agency, scientific-society and arXiv texts on orbital compute, 2019-2026, found by web discovery searches and direct retrieval); not a claim that no earlier text exists. From a proponent, which states the challenge while arguing the concept is not precluded by physics; the paper states that cooling would be achieved through heat pipes and radiators.

  > “the core concepts of space-based ML compute are not precluded by fundamental physics or insurmountable economic barriers. However, significant engineering challenges remain, such as thermal management, high-bandwidth ground communications, and on-orbit system reliability”  
  > [Exploring a space-based, scalable AI infrastructure system design](https://research.google/blog/exploring-a-space-based-scalable-ai-infrastructure-system-design/), conclusion, paragraph 1
  > “Cooling would be achieved through a thermal system of heat pipes and radiators while operating at nominal temperatures.”  
  > [Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (arXiv:2511.19468v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.19468v1), PDF p. 2 · context

- **Turyshev's preprint (April 2026) treats radiator area as part of its base-case mass model, with about 2,500 m2 of radiator for 1 MW of IT power, and concludes that general compute for terrestrial users closes only when several conditions hold together.** `cpo-orb-oc6-turyshev-2026`
  2026-04-29 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: contesting
  _Limits:_ Independent preprint; the radiator figure is its modelled base case, not a measurement.

  > [Orbital Data Centers: Spacecraft Constraints and Economic Viability (arXiv:2604.27197v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.27197v1), abstract
  > “terrestrial-user general compute closes only for low Earth-coupled communication intensity, high effective utilization, long delivered lifetime, and very low combined launch-plus-build cost”  
  > [Orbital Data Centers: Spacecraft Constraints and Economic Viability (arXiv:2604.27197v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.27197v1), abstract

### OC7 Radiation effects

#### Arguments

- **The Lumen Orbit white paper (September 2024) states that shielding is required against radiation-induced latch-up, transients and total-dose effects in sensitive components, while arguing that shielding mass per unit of compute falls with container size.** `cpo-orb-oc7-lumen-2024`
  2024-09 · published · US · `articulates_proposition` · role: earliest-located
  _Limits:_ Earliest located among the texts retrieved for this strand on 2026-10-28 (company, agency, scientific-society and arXiv texts on orbital compute, 2019-2026, found by web discovery searches and direct retrieval); not a claim that no earlier text exists. Pre-2024 space-electronics radiation literature (for example on ISS computing experiments) was not searched. From a proponent, which treats radiation as a manageable design cost.

  > “shielding is required to reduce radiation-induced effects, including latch-up, transients, and total ionizing dose (TID) effects in sensitive components such as storage and power delivery”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 10, "Orbit"
  > “Therefore the mass of shielding needed per compute unit decreases linearly with container size.”  
  > [Why we should train AI in space (White Paper v1.01)](https://lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf), PDF p. 11, "Orbit"

- **The Suncatcher paper (November 2025) reports proton-beam tests in which Trillium TPU HBM showed irregularities after 2 krad(Si), about three times the authors' five-year requirement of 750 rad(Si).** `cpo-orb-oc7-suncatcher-paper-2025`
  2025-11-22 · published · US · `bears_on_proposition` · role: context
  _Limits:_ A proponent's ground test result, recorded as context: it shows radiation effects and a margin, not in-orbit performance.

  > “In our tests, HBM-specific stress tests began to show irregularities after a cumulative dose of 2 krad(Si), which is almost 3× our minimum requirement for TID survival of 750 rad(Si).”  
  > [Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (arXiv:2511.19468v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.19468v1), PDF p. 5, radiation testing

- **Cavalier's IEEE Spectrum article (June 2026) argues that commercial AI chips are "soft" targets for high-energy particles in orbit, which can flip memory bits or cause latch-ups.** `cpo-orb-oc7-cavalier-2026`
  2026-06-11 · published · Other · `articulates_proposition` · role: contesting
  _Limits:_ Analyst's argument; it also describes redundancy as a mitigation.
  > “The problem is that these chips are “soft” targets in space. High-energy particles can flip bits in memory or cause “latch-ups” in logic that fry the circuit.”  
  > [Why Thermodynamics Rules Future Orbital Data Centers](https://spectrum.ieee.org/orbital-data-centers-heat), section "The Silicon Challenge in Space"

  > [Towards a future space-based, highly scalable AI infrastructure system design (arXiv:2511.19468v1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.19468v1), PDF p. 1, arXiv stamp

### Three-Body Computing Constellation (三体计算星座)

#### Announcements, plans and agreements

- **Science and Technology Daily, relayed on ncsti.gov.cn, reported Zhejiang Lab as stating that the constellation's first mission reached an overall in-orbit computing power of 5 POPS and had deployed and verified 10 AI models and applications in orbit.** `cpo-orb-three-body-capacity-stated`
  2026-02-12 · stated operating · Orbit · **press only** · `compute_capacity`
  _Date:_ Date on which the report says the reporter learned this from Zhejiang Lab; the relay is dated 13 February 2026.
  _Figures and stated dates:_ quantity 5 POPS (as worded: '5POPS') (measures: computing throughput as stated)
  _Limits:_ Press-only, attributed to Zhejiang Lab; POPS is the lab's unit (peta operations per second) with no precision or benchmark stated. Ranking wording in the report is not adopted.

  > “通过在轨协同完成了10个人工智能（AI）模型与应用的部署与验证” — _working translation:_ “through in-orbit coordination, completed the deployment and verification of 10 artificial intelligence (AI) models and applications (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [三体计算星座编织全球最强太空算力网](https://www.ncsti.gov.cn/kjdt/kjrd/202602/t20260213_238278.html), paragraph 1 · secondary source
  > “整体在轨算力达5POPS” — _working translation:_ “overall in-orbit computing power reached 5 POPS (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [三体计算星座编织全球最强太空算力网](https://www.ncsti.gov.cn/kjdt/kjrd/202602/t20260213_238278.html), final paragraph · secondary source

- **Science and Technology Daily, relayed on ncsti.gov.cn, described the Three-Body Computing Constellation as a thousand-satellite-scale space computing infrastructure that Zhejiang Lab is building with global partners, and stated that its first 12 computing satellites entered orbit on 14 May 2025.** `cpo-orb-three-body-thousand-plan`
  2026-02-13 · published · China · **press only** · proposed predicate `announced_plan`
  _Date:_ Relay date.
  _Limits:_ Press-only; "千星规模" (thousand-satellite scale) is a description of the planned scale, with no date stated in this text.
  > “三体计算星座是之江实验室协同全球合作伙伴共同打造的千星规模太空计算基础设施” — _working translation:_ “The Three-Body Computing Constellation is a thousand-satellite-scale space computing infrastructure built by Zhejiang Lab together with global partners. (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [三体计算星座编织全球最强太空算力网](https://www.ncsti.gov.cn/kjdt/kjrd/202602/t20260213_238278.html), paragraph 2 · secondary source
  > “2025年5月14日，三体计算星座首发12颗计算卫星成功入轨” — _working translation:_ “On 14 May 2025, the first 12 computing satellites of the Three-Body Computing Constellation successfully entered orbit. (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [三体计算星座编织全球最强太空算力网](https://www.ncsti.gov.cn/kjdt/kjrd/202602/t20260213_238278.html), paragraph 2 · secondary source

#### Stated status

- **ADA Space states that group 01 of its "Xingsuan" (星算) programme, a space computing centre, was launched into orbit in May 2025; Xinhua reported the launch of a space computing satellite constellation from Jiuquan on 14 May 2025.** `cpo-orb-three-body-launch-stated`
  2025-05-14 · launch (as stated) · Orbit · proposed predicate `launch_stated`
  _Date:_ Day from Xinhua (gov.cn); ADA Space's homepage gives the month only.
  _Limits:_ The operator's statement gives the month; the day rests on Xinhua. Neither text names the Three-Body constellation; the link of the 12 satellites to Zhejiang Lab's constellation rests on press relays (cpo-orb-three-body-composition). ADA Space's ranking wording ("位居全球第一") is not adopted.

  > “2025年5月，国星宇航“星算”计划01组太空计算中心成功发射入轨” — _working translation:_ “In May 2025, ADA Space's "Xingsuan" programme group 01 space computing centre was successfully launched into orbit. (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [国星宇航 · 让算力，在太空运行](http://www.adaspace.com/), company profile, paragraph 2
  > “5月14日12时12分” — _working translation:_ “At 12:12 on 14 May (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [我国成功发射太空计算卫星星座](https://www.gov.cn/yaowen/tupian/202505/content_7023750.htm), photo caption (repeated) · secondary source
  > “成功将太空计算卫星星座发射升空” — _working translation:_ “successfully launched the space computing satellite constellation (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [我国成功发射太空计算卫星星座](https://www.gov.cn/yaowen/tupian/202505/content_7023750.htm), photo caption (repeated) · secondary source
  > the launch was from the Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center (酒泉卫星发射中心) on a Long March 2D rocket. _(paraphrase)_  
  > [我国成功发射太空计算卫星星座](https://www.gov.cn/yaowen/tupian/202505/content_7023750.htm), photo caption

- **A press report relayed on ncsti.gov.cn on 15 May 2025 states that the space computing constellation consists of 12 satellites built by ADA Space and carries Zhejiang Lab's onboard intelligent computers and space-based models.** `cpo-orb-three-body-composition`

  > “太空计算卫星星座是“星算”计划首发星座，由国星宇航研制的12颗卫星组成” — _working translation:_ “The space computing satellite constellation is the first constellation of the "Xingsuan" plan, consisting of 12 satellites developed by ADA Space. (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [全球首个太空计算星座成功发射](https://www.ncsti.gov.cn/kjdt/kjrd/qtrd_kjrd/202505/t20250515_204850.html), paragraph 2 · secondary source
  > “还搭载了之江实验室研制的星载智能计算机等太空计算软硬件和天基模型” — _working translation:_ “[the satellites] also carry space computing hardware and software, such as onboard intelligent computers developed by Zhejiang Lab, and space-based models. (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [全球首个太空计算星座成功发射](https://www.ncsti.gov.cn/kjdt/kjrd/qtrd_kjrd/202505/t20250515_204850.html), paragraph 2 · secondary source

- **ADA Space states that in October 2026 it launched eight satellites of group 02 of its "Xingsuan" programme into a dawn-dusk orbit on a Zhuque-2E (朱雀二号改进型) Y4 rocket from the Dongfeng commercial space innovation test zone.** `cpo-orb-xingsuan-02-launch-stated`
  2026-10 · launch (as stated) · Orbit · proposed predicate `launch_stated`
  _Date:_ Month as stated in the homepage company profile read on 2026-10-28; the homepage news list has an item dated 2026.10.14 on a one-rocket, eight-satellite launch (国星宇航一箭八星发射任务取得圆满成功).
  _Limits:_ Operator statement only, month precision. The text does not say whether group 02 belongs to the Three-Body constellation or what computing it carries.
  > “成功将“星算”计划02组星座晨昏轨道8颗卫星发射升空” — _working translation:_ “successfully launched the eight dawn-dusk-orbit satellites of the "Xingsuan" plan group 02 constellation (Selekoa working translation)”  
  > [国星宇航 · 让算力，在太空运行](http://www.adaspace.com/), company profile, paragraph 2

  > [国星宇航 · 让算力，在太空运行](http://www.adaspace.com/), company profile, paragraph 2

- `power_agreement`: 19 records

## Omissions

Listed with reasons. An omission is not a judgement of importance.

- **BIS “Export Controls on Semiconductor Manufacturing Items” IFR (25 October 2023) and the October 2022 advanced computing IFR** (chips). Bounds: the 2023 equipment rule does not address AI compute in the passages read; the 2022 rule is before the period and is named only as the rule revised (cpo-ch-bis-acr-2023-published).
- **BIS Data Center Validated End User rule (2 October 2024), Entity List rules (2023–2025) and the April 2026 extension of authorized IC designer status** (chips). Bounds; found in the Federal Register search but not read. Candidates for a later pass.
- **AI diffusion rule, its ECCN 4E091 provision, the September 2024 reporting proposal and the May 2025 rescission announcement** (chips). Already in the dataset (fl-us-bis-*); not re-recorded. A Federal Register search on 2026-10-28 for BIS documents mentioning “Artificial Intelligence Diffusion” from March 2025 found only the July 2026 UAE rule; no rescission rule was located (see openQuestions).
- **Reported August 2025 arrangement for NVIDIA and AMD to pay the US government a share of China chip revenue; President Trump’s December 2025 announcement on H200 exports** (chips). No primary text retrieved (press reporting only); the January 2026 BIS rule is recorded instead.
- **Reported February 2026 change to the NVIDIA–OpenAI arrangement (an investment of about $30 billion in OpenAI’s funding round)** (chips). Press reporting only; NVIDIA’s fourth-quarter fiscal 2026 release (retrieved) does not mention OpenAI. Left as an open question.
- **Completion of NVIDIA’s $5 billion Intel investment** (chips). No primary text retrieved on closing; sec.gov and newsroom.intel.com refuse.
- **AMD CFO’s statement that the OpenAI agreement is expected to deliver tens of billions of dollars in revenue** (chips). A projection about AMD’s revenue rather than chip supply; left out for bounds.
- **Other CHIPS awards (Samsung, Micron, SK hynix packaging, Intel’s November 2024 award) and the White House May 2025 US–UAE AI cooperation fact sheet** (chips). Bounds; not retrieved. The UAE framework is cited by the July 2026 BIS rule (recorded).
- **SK Telecom–OpenAI MOU on a data centre in South Korea; Samsung SDS, C&T and Heavy Industries elements of the Samsung LOI** (chips). Data-centre or non-chip elements; belong to the data-centre strand.
- **NVIDIA–Foxconn and NVIDIA–Wistron supercomputer plants in Texas (April 2025 post)** (chips). Bounds; the post’s production plan and Arizona chip production are recorded.
- **DeepSeek statements on domestic Chinese chips** (chips). No primary DeepSeek text on domestic chips was located in this pass.
- **MOFCOM Announcement No. 62 of 2025 (rare-earth technologies) and joint Announcements Nos. 55–58 of 2025** (chips). Not retrieved and not read; No. 61 is the one whose text names chips and AI.
- **NVIDIA and Intel announced on 18 September 2025 a collaboration to develop multiple generations of custom data-centre and PC products, under which Intel will build NVIDIA-custom x86 CPUs that NVIDIA will integrate into its AI infrastructure platforms. (drafted as cpo-ch-nvidia-intel-custom-cpus)** (chips). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Product detail of the NVIDIA–Intel collaboration; the investment record is kept.
- **NVIDIA stated on 28 May 2025 that, as a result of the H20 licence requirement, it incurred a $4.5 billion charge in the first quarter of fiscal 2026 for H20 excess inventory and purchase obligations and was unable to ship an additional $2.5 billion of H20 revenue in that quarter. (drafted as cpo-ch-nvidia-h20-charge)** (chips). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Financial charge from the H20 licence requirement; the requirement and the stated end of China sales are kept.
- **Among the conditions for case-by-case review, the January 2026 rule requires certification that aggregate shipments of the product to China and Macau will be no more than 50% of the total shipped to customers for end use in the United States, and that every shipment is reviewed before export by a qualified third-party testing lab in the United States. (drafted as cpo-ch-bis-license-policy-2026-conditions)** (chips). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Licence conditions of the 2026 BIS rule; its publication and case-by-case policy are kept.
- **MOFCOM Announcement No. 72 of 2025, dated 9 November 2025, suspends paragraph 2 of Announcement No. 46 of 2024 from that day until 27 November 2026. (drafted as cpo-ch-cn-mofcom-2025-72-suspension)** (chips). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Second suspension announcement of November 2025; announcement 70/2025 is kept.
- **In the same keynote Huawei stated that it would offer two proprietary HBMs, HiBL 1.0 and HiZQ 2.0, packaged with the Ascend 950 die. (drafted as cpo-ch-huawei-proprietary-hbm)** (chips). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Detail of Huawei’s roadmap; the roadmap record is kept.
- **Huawei stated in the keynote of 18 September 2025 that its Atlas 950 SuperPoD will have up to 8,192 Ascend 950DT chips. (drafted as cpo-ch-huawei-atlas-950-superpod)** (chips). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Detail of Huawei’s roadmap; the roadmap record is kept.
- **OpenAI's own posts on the five Stargate sites, Stargate Norway, Stargate UK, Stargate UAE and Stargate Michigan** (data-centres). openai.com refuses direct retrieval; counterparties' releases used instead. The cross-family checker should compare them.
- **Stargate Argentina (Sur Energy, October 2025), Stargate India and other OpenAI for Countries sites** (data-centres). No reachable primary source located in this pass; not recorded from press reports.
- **Prometheus capacity and online date from Zuckerberg's July 2025 Threads post** (data-centres). Recorded from the earnings-call transcript instead (cpo-dc-meta-prometheus-2026); the Threads post was not retrieved.
- **Colossus 2 (Memphis) site-level capacity** (data-centres). x.ai refuses; no reachable government or NVIDIA text stating Colossus 2 capacity was located. The aggregate ~2GW appears only via the Mississippi release.
- **HUMAIN (Saudi Arabia) data-centre sites** (data-centres). www.humain.com refuses; no other primary source located.
- **Mistral AI's Bruyères-le-Châtel (Essonne) site, 18,000 Grace Blackwell plan and 2026 debt financing** (data-centres). Located only in press reports; Mistral's own posts read here name no site. Not recorded.
- **Alibaba's later capex statements and overseas data centres; other Chinese developer sites** (data-centres). Bounds; no primary text stating a site and capacity was retrieved in this pass. Alibaba's RMB 380bn plan is already in the dataset.
- **Amazon North Carolina (Richmond County, June 2025) and other state investments** (data-centres). Bounds: Pennsylvania recorded as the example with named sites.
- **NVIDIA GB300 supply to Stargate UAE; GPU supply in Stargate Norway and UK** (data-centres). Chip supply belongs to the chips strand.
- **Power arrangements at sites (Meta–Entergy, Microsoft–We Energies, DTE for Saline, Lancium behind-the-meter plan, Meta nuclear agreements)** (data-centres). Power belongs to the power strand; mentioned only in qualifications.
- **Microsoft's statement that Fairwater supports OpenAI and the MAI Superintelligence Team** (data-centres). A statement of intended use across the network, not tied to a site; left for a later pass.
- **EU AI gigafactories formal call, EuroHPC Regulation amendment and any selections** (data-centres). Not retrieved in this pass (digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu refuses); the press corner API may hold later releases.
- **Crusoe–Abilene financing (Blue Owl / Primary Digital joint venture) and Crusoe's Childress and Wyoming campuses** (data-centres). Financing and non-frontier-developer campuses outside the bounds of this pass.
- **Mistral AI's stated plan to build up to 1 GW of capacity by 2030 (mistral.ai post of 11 August 2026)** (data-centres). The sentence appears only in a box headed "Thinking Summary" above the post, possibly machine-generated, and not in the body; not recorded until the checker confirms it is Mistral-authored content.
- **Anthropic–Fluidstack sites in Texas and New York as separate site records; Google Armstrong County campus; Amazon Salem and Falls Township campuses as separate site records** (data-centres). The sources name only a state or township, with no capacity or schedule; covered inside the investment records to keep the strand bounded.
- **Lordstown and Milam County later status statements** (data-centres). No later party statement retrieved.
- **Crusoe announced on 18 March 2025 that construction had begun on the second phase of its Abilene AI data center, six additional buildings expected to be completed in mid-2026. (drafted as cpo-dc-crusoe-abilene-phase2-construction)** (data-centres). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Second-phase construction start; the phase plan and the 2026 energization statement are kept.
- **The release stated that the facility "will target to deliver 100,000 NVIDIA GPUs by the end of 2026". (drafted as cpo-dc-stargate-norway-gpus)** (data-centres). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. GPU target of Stargate Norway; the announcement and its 230 MW record are kept.
- **Microsoft stated on 18 September 2025 that it was in the final phases of building Fairwater in Mount Pleasant, Wisconsin, and on track to bring it online in early 2026. (drafted as cpo-dc-fairwater-wi-final-phases)** (data-centres). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Later-phase detail of Fairwater Wisconsin; the plan and the stated operation of the first facility are kept.
- **Microsoft committed in September 2025 an additional $4 billion, to be spent over three years, to build a second Wisconsin datacenter of similar size, beside its initial $3.3 billion pledge. (drafted as cpo-dc-fairwater-wi-second-4bn)** (data-centres). Dropped at integration to keep the pack within its bound of about 200 records. Spending figure for a second Wisconsin facility; detail beyond the site records kept.

Bounds; two independent critical preprints (Turyshev, Marcy) were read in full for this pass.

- **Earlier space-based solar power literature (e.g. proposals the Suncatcher paper cites)** (orbital). Outside the 2019-2026 search bound and about power transmission to Earth, not compute in orbit.
- **Drafted, then dropped for bounds: NVIDIA's pre-launch statement of Starcloud-1's November 2025 target; SpaceX's Starship V3 (12 May 2026) and Terafab (6 August 2026) updates naming orbital data centres; context records for the 2022 ASCEND announcement (OC1, OC5), Turyshev on OC1, NVIDIA on OC2, Google on OC3 (terrestrial resources), Cowboy Space on OC3 (grid lead times), Google's blog on OC4, Cavalier on OC8, and Axiom's October 2026 bandwidth argument (OC10)** (orbital). The cached texts remain (orb-spacex-updates-api, orb-tas-ascend-2022, orb-aetherflux-home, orb-axiom-kepler-quantum) for a later pass.

## Open questions from the drafting passes

- Proposed predicates: `export_licence_requirement_stated` (organisation → literal; a party states that a government told it a licence is required, or states the effect) is used for three NVIDIA H20 records; `transactionStage` values "term_sheet", "preliminary" and "final" are used for the Broadcom term sheet and CHIPS awards. _Recommended:_ Accept `export_licence_requirement_stated` or fold the records into `states_provision` once a BIS letter or rule is retrieved; add the three stage values to the proposed enum alongside "letter_of_intent". (chips)
- Did the OpenAI–NVIDIA letter of intent of 22 September 2025 become a definitive agreement, and is NVIDIA’s “up to $100 billion” intent still stated? Press reports of February 2026 describe a different investment of about $30 billion. _Recommended:_ The cross-family checker should look for an NVIDIA or OpenAI primary statement (NVIDIA 10-K for fiscal 2026 or a capture of OpenAI’s post). Until then keep both records as of September 2025 and add no later status. (chips)
- Was the AI diffusion rule’s rescission formalised in the Federal Register? A search of BIS documents from March 2025 mentioning “Artificial Intelligence Diffusion” found only the July 2026 UAE rule. _Recommended:_ Leave the dataset record (fl-us-bis-ai-diffusion-rescission-announced) as an announcement; ask the checker to search the Federal Register by RIN 0694-AJ90 or docket before stating any formal rescission. Integrator's note: the July 2026 UAE rule states that the worldwide licence requirement is still specified in the EAR and that BIS enforces it only for Country Groups D:1, D:4 and D:5, consistent with the May 2025 guidance (cpo-ch-bis-uae-2026-diffusion-enforcement); this supports keeping the rescission as announced. (chips)
- The GP10 guidance prints “Huawei Ascent 910D”. Should the record normalise it to Ascend? _Recommended:_ Keep the printed form in the quotation and the normalised reading in the statement, flagged as Selekoa’s reading (as done). (chips)
- Intel’s release gives $20.47 per share; the 8-K gives $20.74 (issued shares, if all released funds are received) and $20.00 (escrowed shares). Which should a timeline show? _Recommended:_ Show the 8-K terms (filed) and keep the release figure in its own record with the discrepancy in the qualification (as done). (chips)
- Region coding for the Samsung and SK hynix LOIs (Korean companies, OpenAI’s Stargate) and for the H20/H200 records (US rules on exports to China). _Recommended:_ Use "Other" for the Korean memory LOIs and "China" for records whose subject matter is exports to China, keeping "US" for the publication records of US rules; the integrator may prefer one rule across strands. (chips)
- Should Crusoe's Abilene campus (crusoe-abilene-campus) be merged with the dataset's stargate-abilene? _Recommended:_ Not yet. Crusoe's releases read here never name Stargate, OpenAI or Oracle. Merge only when a single primary text (for example OpenAI's or Oracle's, via a capture) names Crusoe as the developer of Stargate I. (data-centres)
- Two predicates are proposed beyond the shared list: designated_zone (public_body → infrastructure, a government designation of a zone of sites) and programme_status_stated (public_body → literal, a programme's stated state of progress). Adopt them? _Recommended:_ Adopt designated_zone for government designations; fold programme_status_stated into site_status_stated generalised to programmes, or adopt it if the schema keeps sites and programmes apart. (data-centres)
- Some statedDates kinds are used beyond FORMAT's list (target_completion, construction_start_target). _Recommended:_ Accept both: target_completion for stated completion schedules, construction_start_target for planned starts, so that a stated start (construction_start_stated) stays distinct from a planned one. (data-centres)
- The Nscale and UK government texts word OpenAI's Stargate UK GPU uptake differently ("explore offtake" vs "take"). _Recommended:_ Keep one record with both passages and the difference in the qualification; the checker should open OpenAI's "Introducing Stargate UK" post for its own wording. (data-centres)
- Do the orbital arguments (OC1-OC11) belong in the thought spine (propositions) or the governance spine? _Recommended:_ Thought spine, as propositions with document subjects and earliest-located roles, as the scope already proposes: they are arguments made in papers, white papers and letters, and the propositions model exists for them. The governance spine (Frontier AI governance) should hold the regulatory records - the FCC applications, acceptances and the Lincei and Secure World Foundation submissions - and link to the propositions those submissions argue. Two records carry both roles (the Lincei comment and the SWF post); keep them as argument records here and cross-link from governance rather than duplicate. (orbital)
- Proposed predicates beyond the shared list: operation_stated (organisation -> infrastructure: an operator states a spacecraft carrying compute is operating or has run a stated workload) and accepted_for_filing (public_body -> document: a regulator accepts an application for filing). _Recommended:_ Adopt operation_stated as the spacecraft analogue of site_status_stated, or merge both into one status predicate with a subject-type rule; adopt accepted_for_filing, because acceptance is a procedural step that must not be read as approval (the record now uses time.basis 'approval' for want of a 'procedural' basis - consider adding one). (orbital)
- Is Aetherflux now Cowboy Space Corporation? _Recommended:_ Treat as unverified: aetherflux.com redirects to cowboyspace.com, and NVIDIA's March 2026 release still names Aetherflux. Cross-family checker: look for a primary rename statement before linking the entities. (orbital)
- The Chinese constellation records rest on Xinhua (gov.cn) and ncsti.gov.cn relays of press reports, plus ADA Space's homepage. Should they stay press-only? _Recommended:_ Yes, until a Zhejiang Lab or ADA Space release is retrieved. Cross-family checker: open zhejianglab.com news for 14 May 2025 and 12-13 February 2026, and yuhang.gov.cn art_1532122_59140829. (orbital)
- IEEE Spectrum article by an ABI Research analyst: press-only or primary for the argument it states? _Recommended:_ Primary for 'the article argues', pressOnly false: it is the author's own signed analysis, not reporting of others. Flag if the maintainer reads 'press-only' as covering all magazine texts. (orbital)
- Which Starcloud white paper version is canonical for the earliest-located OC records? _Recommended:_ v1.01 (Lumen Orbit, Inc.), still served at lumenorbit.github.io; its September 2024 date is printed but attested only by the 2026 copy. Cross-family checker: open an Internet Archive capture of lumenorbit.github.io/wp.pdf or lumenorbit.com from 2024 to attest the date (the availability service was rate-limited here). (orbital)
- Earliest-located labels for OC6, OC8 and OC10 fall on proponents' texts (Google's Suncatcher), which state these limits while arguing the concept is viable. _Recommended:_ Keep them, with roles ['earliest-located', 'contesting'] and the proponent noted in each qualification; a later search of pre-2024 engineering literature on space computing may well find earlier texts and should be recorded as a correction. (orbital)
- Should regulator and authority decisions (FERC, NRC, LPSC, Shelby County permit) use a proposed predicate `regulator_decision` (public_body → literal), given that none of the shared predicates fits? _Recommended:_ Yes: adopt `regulator_decision` across strands, with the decision's own date as event date only where a primary record of the decision was retrieved; otherwise date by the reporting statement, as done here. (power)
- The UK AI Energy Council record uses a proposed predicate `convened` (public_body → literal). Keep, or fold into `announced_plan`? _Recommended:_ Keep `convened` for the first meeting of a standing body; it is not a plan. (power)
- Did FERC act on the Secretary of Energy's 23 October 2025 large-load ANOPR (reported docket RM26-4-000) by 30 April 2026? _Recommended:_ Ask the cross-family checker to open the FERC eLibrary docket or a capture of ferc.gov; record any order as a separate `regulator_decision`, without assuming adoption. (power)
- Talen–Amazon: the cross-family checker should open Talen's 11 June 2025 release (ir.talenenergy.com or a capture) to replace the press-only evidence, and FERC's November 2024 order to record its date and docket. _Recommended:_ Keep the records press-only until a primary text is checked; do not promote. (power)
- Crane restart target: Constellation stated 2028 (September 2024) and 2027 (August 2026). Should the timeline show both? _Recommended:_ Yes, as two stated targets attributed and dated by their statements; neither is an operation date. (power)
- Chinese 2026 plan evidence rests on a provincial-government reprint (hlj.gov.cn) and NDA's republication. Is that acceptable as primary? _Recommended:_ Acceptable as an official copy, labelled as such; the checker should compare with the NEA original (nea.gov.cn, refused here) or the gov.cn policy library. (power)
- statedDates kinds outside the FORMAT examples were used (deadline_stated, contract_end_stated, delivery_start_stated, selection_target, target_stated). Normalise at merge? _Recommended:_ Normalise at merge to a short enum: target_operation, delivery_target, delivery_start_stated, deadline_stated, target_stated. (power)
